## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, p.7, p.8, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 1B-1, once FHWA approves the request ("Approved?" → Yes), the next step is that the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. From there, the process branches into evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to the FHWA Division & HQ, ultimately concluding with the jurisdiction providing FHWA a copy of the final report. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." (This is why installation occurs only after approval in the flowchart.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 1B-1, where the "Yes" branch from the "Approved?" decision diamond leads directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then branches to "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ," both converging on "Requesting jurisdiction provides FHWA a copy of final report." [Image 5] shows the same Figure 1B-1 flowchart in the full-page context, confirming the sequence following approval. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Support §9 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Page 48

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.07, Section 1B.06, Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, Figure 1B-1, p.9. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.33.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, Figure 1B-2 shows a formal rulemaking sequence: FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes that Notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews the comments received, and then prepares the Final Rule before publishing it. After publication of the Final Rule, State manuals must be brought into substantial conformance with the National MUTCD within 2 years. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]." Visual evidence: - [Image 2] Figure 1B-2 shows the rulemaking branch after "Accepted for Federal rulemaking? – YES": FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments → FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in Federal Register → Docket comment period → FHWA reviews comments → FHWA prepares Final Rule → FHWA publishes Final Rule → State manuals must be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD within 2 years as specified in 23 CFR 655.603(a). - [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 depicts the separate experimentation process that can generate the "request for change based on successful experimentation" feeding into Figure 1B-2's FHWA review. Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Section 1B.06 Support §4 (p.9) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a shoulder wider than 6 feet, Figure 2A-2 (diagram A) shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. (The 6-foot offset from the edge of the shoulder in diagram B applies only where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet.) Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - "For post-mounted signs, the minimum lateral offset should be 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. If a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, the minimum lateral offset for post-mounted signs should be 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, diagram A ("Roadside sign in rural area") depicts a STOP sign with "5 ft MIN." vertical height and "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way, while diagram B shows the alternative "6 ft MIN." offset only where the shoulder is wider than 6 ft. - [Image 5] The full-page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms diagram A's dimensions of 5 ft minimum height and 12 ft minimum lateral offset for the rural roadside sign. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Guidance §3 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51)

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

## F007

**Question:** What visual feature distinguishes the 'separate intersections' case from the 'single intersection' case in Figure 2A-5?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.23, Figure 2A-5, p.63, p.64. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinguishing feature is the behavior of the blue arrows showing the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles. In Panel A ("Separate intersections"), the median is wide enough that the two opposing left-turn paths cross each other within the median area, while in Panel B ("Single intersection"), the narrower median means the opposing left-turn paths curve away from each other and do not cross. This left-turn path interaction, together with interior storage in the median, determines whether the crossing functions as one or two intersections. Guidance (recommended): - "A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: A. The paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and B. There is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway." - "At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows Panel A ("Separate intersections – paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross") with a wide median where the two blue left-turn paths intersect each other, and Panel B ("Single intersection – paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross") with a narrow median where the blue left-turn paths curve without intersecting. - [Image 5] The full page of Figure 2A-5 (Sect. 2A.23) confirms the two-panel comparison and the legend distinguishing "Direction of travel" from "Path of left-turning vehicle," along with the Support text noting that median widths between 30 and 85 feet may function as either one or two intersections. Citations: - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Figure 2A-5 (p.64)

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque has a white legend and border on a red background. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the octagonal red STOP (R1-1) sign, the downward-pointing triangular YIELD (R1-2) sign, and the horizontal rectangular ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque with white legend and border on a red background. **Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

The figure labels the Walk Interval as "7 seconds MIN." and the Buffer Interval as "2 seconds MIN." Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement. The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Options (permitted): - Per the figure notes: the countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less; the Walk Interval may be reduced under some conditions (see Section 4I.06); and the Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing hand with countdown) → Buffer Interval (steady hand), with "7 seconds MIN.**" labeled on the Walk Interval and "2 seconds MIN." labeled on the Buffer Interval, plus the "zero" point of the countdown display at the start of the Buffer Interval. [Image 5] Page 765 confirms the same Figure 4I-4 in page context, including its footnotes and the relationships to associated vehicular phase intervals. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Standard §4 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 765

**Gold answer:** Walk interval, pedestrian change interval, then buffer interval. The walk interval is labeled 7 seconds minimum and the buffer interval 2 seconds minimum, subject to the stated walk-interval exception.

The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The principal difference is one of right-of-way and relationship to the roadway. In panel A of Figure 8E-1, the pathway crosses the tracks within its own independent pathway right-of-way, separated from any roadway; in panel B, the sidewalk runs parallel to and immediately adjacent to a roadway within the highway right-of-way, so it crosses the tracks alongside the roadway. Because the sidewalk is close to the roadway's traveled way, the roadway crossing's traffic control devices can influence sidewalk users, whereas they cannot adequately warn users of a separated pathway. Standards (mandatory): - "Detectable warnings (see Chapter 3C) shall be used at pathway grade crossings where pedestrian travel is permitted and at sidewalk grade crossings and shall extend across the full width of the pathway or sidewalk." (Both panels of Figure 8E-1 show detectable warnings on each approach.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 8E-1 shows panel A with a pathway in its own labeled "Pathway right-of-way" crossing the railroad independently of any roadway, and panel B with a sidewalk running beside a roadway within the labeled "Highway right-of-way," both with detectable warnings on each side of the tracks within the railroad right-of-way. [Image 5] and [Image 7] show the full-page context of Figure 8E-1 and the accompanying Section 8E.01 Support text explaining that a pathway is frequently placed in its own right-of-way independent of any roadway, while a sidewalk runs parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way. Citations: - Section 8E.01 Support §4 (p.1035) - Section 8E.02 Support §4 (p.1036) - Section 8E.04 Standard §7 (p.1038) - Figure 8E-1 (p.1035) - Page 1076 - Page 1077

**Gold answer:** The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tracks with the roadway.

## M001

**Question:** A provision appears under a Guidance heading and uses the word 'should.' Is it mandatory, and when may a deviation be appropriate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, p.13, p.32. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, a Guidance provision is not mandatory. By definition, Guidance is a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, and deviations are allowed when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates the deviation to be appropriate. Only Standard provisions (using "shall") are mandatory. Standards (mandatory): - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - By contrast, "Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device," in which "The verb 'shall' is typically used." Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) Note: Section 1D.03 (Support) explains that the application of engineering study and engineering judgment is a fundamental principle of the use of traffic control devices, which is the mechanism by which deviations from Guidance (and, in limited documented cases, even from Standards) are determined to be appropriate. - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32)

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The use of a retroreflective strip on a sign support is a permitted conspicuity-enhancement method, but once used it must meet strict requirements: the strip must be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign down to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign's background color (with red required for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs), and carry no legend or other information. A related mandatory application exists at passive grade crossings, where a white retroreflective strip is required on the back of Crossbuck supports. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." - At passive grade crossings: "A vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used on each Crossbuck support at passive grade crossings for the full length of the back of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway... A white retroreflective strip wrapped around a round support... shall satisfy this requirement as long as the round support has an outside diameter of at least 2 inches." Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, where improved conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, any of the methods illustrated in Figure 2A-1 may be used, including adding a strip of retroreflective material to the sign support. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, Example E, illustrates a vertical retroreflective strip (matching the fluorescent yellow-green background of a pedestrian warning sign) running down the support and terminating with a "2 ft MAX." dimension above the near edge of the roadway. [Image 3] Figure 8B-2 shows the grade-crossing application, depicting an optional 2-inch red or white retroreflective strip on the front of the Crossbuck support and the required 2-inch white retroreflective strip on the back of the support, extending to within 2 feet of the roadway edge. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 8B.04 Standard §17 (p.1000) - Section 2A.21 Support §8 (p.62) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Figure 8B-2 (p.997)

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For contrast, in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely, "the minimum height... of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - The figure note accompanying the standardization-of-location provisions states: "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way. See Section 2A.16 for lower minimums that may be used in urban areas, or where lateral offset space is limited." Options (permitted): - "On conventional, low-volume rural, and special-purpose roads in areas where it is impractical to locate a sign with the lateral offset prescribed by this Section because of roadside features such as terrain or vegetation, a lateral offset of at least 2 feet may be used." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 diagrams A and B show a rural roadside sign with "5 ft MIN." height, and lateral offsets of "12 ft MIN." from the traveled way (no shoulder wider than 6 ft) or "6 ft MIN." from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet. - [Image 2] Figure 2A-3 restates in its note that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. - [Image 5] shows the full published page containing Figure 2A-2 with the 5-foot height and 12-foot/6-foot lateral offset dimensions for rural roadside signs. - [Image 7] shows Section 2A.15 text (5-foot rural minimum context) and the beginning of Section 2A.16 Lateral Offset. - [Image 8] shows the full published page containing Figure 2A-3 with the 6-to-12-foot lateral offset dimensions at intersections. **Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below each STOP sign. It has a white legend and border on a red background. A 4-WAY or other numeric-way plaque may not be substituted.

## M006

**Question:** Compare the quantitative triggers in the four-leg All-Way Stop crash warrant and the 8-hour volume warrant.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

**Rationale:** The response received 93.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.13, Section 2B.16, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. | The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a p… Adjudication note: Correct crash and eight-hour volume thresholds; omits the 70-percent high-speed adjustment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.624 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Both warrants are permissive (Option) criteria for installing all-way stop control based on an engineering study, but they use different metrics. The crash warrant for a four-leg intersection is triggered by five or more reported correctable crashes in 12 months, or six or more in 36 months. The 8-hour volume warrant is triggered by combined vehicle/bicycle/pedestrian entering volumes of at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor-street approaches, sustained for each of the same 8 hours of a typical day. Options (permitted): - Crash warrant (four-leg): All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates "there are five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control." (By comparison, the three-leg thresholds are four crashes in 12 months or five in 36 months.) - 8-hour volume warrant: All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates (A) "the combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day" and (B) "the combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." - Key comparison: the crash warrant counts discrete safety events over long observation windows (12 or 36 months) limited to crash types correctable by all-way stop control, while the volume warrant counts multimodal demand (vehicles + bicycles + pedestrians combined) that must be met simultaneously on major and minor streets during the same 8 hours of a single typical day. Citations: - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a possible 70-percent adjustment if major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The mandatory requirement is to have and use an assessment or management method designed to maintain retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums — not that every individual sign meet the minimums at all times. The MUTCD's Support text expressly states that an agency using such a method remains in compliance even if some individual signs are temporarily below the minimum levels; those identified as deficient should be replaced. For white-on-red (STOP-type) signs, Table 2A-5 sets minimum maintained levels of W ≥ 35 and R ≥ 7 cd/lx/m², with a required minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity). Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." - Table 2A-5 footnote 4 specifies for white-on-red signs a "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)"; retroreflectivity values are in cd/lx/m² measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of −4.0°. Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums; "Signs that are identified through the agency's method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: (A) Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; (B) Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; (C) Acknowledgment signs; and (D) Bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. Support (explanatory): - "Compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section is achieved by having a method in place and using the method to maintain the minimum levels established in Table 2A-5. Provided that an assessment or management method is being used, an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, Minimum Maintained Retroreflectivity Levels, including the white-on-red row (W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7) applicable to STOP-type signs along with the 3:1 contrast-ratio footnote. [Image 5] shows the full context of Section 2A.22, including the Standard (§2), the Support statement on compliance with individual deficient signs (§3), the Guidance on methods and replacement (§4), and the Option exclusions (§5). Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Support §3 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62) - Page 103

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. On a roadway posted at 35 mph, sizes smaller than the prescribed (Conventional Road) dimensions are not permitted, because reduced/Minimum sizes are restricted to low-speed facilities with operating speeds of 30 mph or less, and on multi-lane conventional roads the minimum size is the Conventional Road size itself. However, the evidence provided does not include the specific regulatory sign size table (e.g., the STOP sign R1-1 dimensions), so the exact required STOP sign dimension cannot be stated from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The sign dimensions prescribed in the sign size tables in the various Parts and Chapters in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05) shall be used unless engineering judgment determines that other sizes are appropriate in accordance with the following." - "Where engineering judgment determines that sizes smaller than the prescribed dimensions are appropriate for use, the sign dimensions shall not be less than the minimum dimensions specified in this Manual." - "The sizes shown in the Minimum columns that are smaller than the sizes shown in the Conventional Road columns in the various sign size tables in this Manual shall only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, and on low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; and only where the reduced legend size would be adequate for the regulation or warning or where physical conditions preclude the use of larger sizes." A 35 mph multi-lane roadway exceeds this 30 mph threshold, so reduced sizes may not be used there. - Note 3 to Table 7B-1: "Minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column." Guidance (recommended): - For school-area signs, "The sizes in the Oversized column should be used on roadways that have four or more lanes with posted speed limits of 40 mph or higher" — at 35 mph this oversized recommendation is not triggered, but the Conventional Road sizes still govern as the minimum for multi-lane roads. - Table 2D-2 (guide sign lettering) distinguishes Single-Lane from Multi-Lane columns by speed range (Less than 30 mph, 30–40 mph, Greater than 40 mph); on a multi-lane road at 35 mph, the Multi-Lane 30–40 mph column applies, not the Single-Lane column. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2D-2 shows separate Single-Lane and Multi-Lane columns for the 30–40 mph speed range, confirming that multi-lane roads use their own (generally larger) letter/numeral sizes rather than single-lane values. - [Image 2] Table 7B-1 includes the note that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column, showing that reduced Minimum-column sizes are not available on multi-lane roads. - [Image 3] shows the Section 2A.07 Standard text restricting Minimum-column sizes to low-speed roadways with operating speeds of 30 mph or less. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 7B.01 Guidance §3 (p.972) - Section 2D.07 Guidance §5 (p.210) - Table 2D-2 (p.210) - Table 7B-1 (p.971) Note on insufficiency: The specific dimension required for a STOP sign (R1-1) on a multi-lane conventional road is prescribed in the regulatory sign size table (Table 2B-1), which is not included in the evidence provided; therefore, the exact STOP sign size cannot be quoted here. What can be stated from the evidence is that the Conventional Road (not Minimum/reduced) size must be used on a 35 mph multi-lane roadway.

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a 55-mph posted or 85th-percentile speed, Table 2C-3 lists an advance placement distance of 495 feet for the potential stop condition (Condition B, deceleration to 0 mph — the column applicable to Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs). No, this distance is not an inflexible mandate: the table is expressly titled "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs," its footnotes describe adjustments (e.g., adding a minimum of 100 feet for signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words), and the surrounding Section 2C.04 provisions are Guidance, calling for engineering judgment and periodic evaluation. However, where a Stop Ahead sign is required (e.g., at certain grade crossings), a Standard does direct that its location be "in accordance with Table 2C-3." Standards (mandatory): - "The Stop Ahead (W3-1), Yield Ahead (W3-2), and Signal Ahead (W3-3) Advance Traffic Control signs (see Figure 2C-8) shall be installed on an approach to a primary traffic control device that is not visible for a sufficient distance to permit the road user to respond to the device (see Table 2C-3)." - At passive grade crossings with a YIELD or STOP sign: "The Yield Ahead or Stop Ahead sign shall be located in accordance with Table 2C-3. The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - The Table 2C-3 row for 55 mph shows: Condition A = 990 ft; Condition B potential stop (0 mph column) = 495 ft; with decreasing values (275 ft, 225 ft, 200 ft, 125 ft, N/A) for higher advisory speeds. Footnote 1 adds that "warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility of the warning sign." - "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions," and the table itself is presented as "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs" — i.e., recommended values, not fixed requirements. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows the 55-mph row with 495 ft in the "0³" (potential stop) column of Condition B, with footnote 3 identifying this column's typical condition as "the warning of a potential stop situation" based on AASHTO stopping sight distance. [Image 4] The full page 152 view shows Section 2C.04, where the Guidance text states warning signs should provide an adequate PRT and the distances in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment, confirming the table's advisory character. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001)

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At a 55-mph curve on a roadway with center line markings, no-passing zone markings are mandatory wherever the passing sight distance falls below the minimum value in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit (the specific 55-mph sight-distance value from Table 3B-1 is not included in the evidence provided). If the gap to the next no-passing zone is less than 400 feet, the recommended practice is to connect the two zones with continuous no-passing zone markings. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** The no-passing zone must be marked when available passing sight distance is less than 900 feet. If successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should connect them.

Meeting the warrant does not compel installation — it only means the need for a signal "should be considered" following an engineering study. Standards (mandatory): - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours that are selected for the Condition A analysis shall not be required to be the same 8 hours that are selected for the Condition B analysis." - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours satisfied in Condition A shall not be required to be the same 8 hours satisfied in Condition B." Guidance (recommended): - The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that, for each of any 8 hours of an average day, the vehicles per hour given in both of the 100 percent columns of Condition A in Table 4C-1 exist on the major street and the more critical minor-street approach, respectively. - Alternatively (under the combination provision), the need for a signal should be considered if, for each of any 8 hours of an average day, the volumes in both of the 80 percent columns of Condition A and both of the 80 percent columns of Condition B are met. - Note: the "should be considered" language means satisfaction of the warrant does not mandate signal installation. Options (permitted): - For signal warrants requiring conditions to be present for a certain number of hours, any four consecutive 15-minute periods may be considered as 1 hour, provided the separate 1-hour periods do not overlap and both the major-street and minor-street volumes are for the same specific 1-hour periods. Citations: - Section 4C.02 Standard §5 (p.653) - Section 4C.02 Standard §10 (p.654) - Section 4C.02 Guidance §4 (p.653) - Section 4C.02 Guidance §9 (p.654) - Section 4C.01 Option §15 (p.652)

**Gold answer:** For each of the same 8 selected hours, 600 vph must exist on the major street (both approaches total) and 150 vph on the critical minor approach (one direction) at the 100-percent thresholds. Meeting the warrant does not compel installation; an engineering study must determine whether a signal is justified.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Warrant 7 (Crash Experience), a four-leg intersection meets the one-year crash criterion if reported angle and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equal or exceed the threshold number in Table 4C-2 — either for total angle and pedestrian crashes (all severities) or for total fatal-and-injury angle and pedestrian crashes (the specific numeric values in Table 4C-2 are not included in the evidence provided). In addition to the crash-history condition, two other categories of conditions must be satisfied: (A) an adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce crash frequency, and (C) a traffic-volume condition applying for each of any 8 hours of an average day (the full text of this volume condition is truncated in the evidence). Guidance (recommended): - The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that all of the following criteria are met: "A. Adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce the crash frequency." - Crash-history condition (at least one must apply): "1. The number of reported angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total angle crashes and pedestrian crashes (all severities); or 2. The number of reported fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes." - A third criterion (C) requires a condition to be met "For each of any 8 hours of an average day"; the remainder of this volume-related provision is cut off in the supplied evidence. Options (permitted): - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, or the intersection lies within the built-up area of an isolated community of less than 10,000 population, Table 4C-4 may be used in place of Table 4C-2 (and the 56 percent volume columns in Table 4C-1 may be used in place of the 80 percent columns). - Agencies may calibrate HSM safety performance functions to their own crash data, or develop their own SPFs, and when documented in the engineering study these agency-specific crash frequency values may be used instead of the values in Tables 4C-2 through 4C-5. Citations: - Section 4C.08 Guidance §2 (p.661) - Section 4C.08 Option §5 (p.662) - Section 4C.08 Option §6 (p.663) - Section 4C.08 Support §1 (p.661) - Section 4C.08 Support §7 (p.663) Note: The evidence does not include the actual numeric threshold values from Table 4C-2, and the text of criterion C (the 8-hour traffic-volume condition) is truncated, so those details cannot be quoted in full. (The crash figures in Section 2B.11 pertain to minor-road stop control, not the Crash Experience signal warrant, and are therefore not applicable here.)

**Gold answer:** The thresholds are at least 5 all-severity angle/pedestrian crashes or at least 3 fatal-and-injury angle/pedestrian crashes. In addition, an adequate trial of alternatives must have failed, and the applicable 80-percent vehicular-volume or pedestrian-volume condition must be met for the required hours.

However, the evidence provided shows Table 4D-2 values only up to 35 mph (325 feet), so the specific distance for a 45 mph 85th-percentile speed cannot be stated from this evidence. If that continuous view is not available, the recommended treatment is to install a signal-ahead warning sign (per Section 2C.35); additionally, on 45 mph approaches, backplates are recommended on all signal faces. Guidance (recommended): - The two primary signal faces required as a minimum for each approach should be continuously visible to approaching traffic from a point at least the minimum sight distance in Table 4D-2 in advance of and measured to the stop line, unless precluded by a physical obstruction or unless another signalized location is within this range. (Note: the excerpt of Table 4D-2 lists distances only for 20–35 mph; the 45 mph value is not included in the evidence.) - If approaching traffic does not have a continuous view of at least two signal faces for at least the minimum sight distance shown in Table 4D-2, a sign (see Section 2C.35) should be installed to warn approaching traffic of the traffic control signal. - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on an approach is 45 mph or higher, signal backplates should be used on all of the signal faces that face the approach. - For approaches with speeds of 45 mph or higher at new or reconstructed installations, additional visibility-related measures are recommended, including locating overhead primary faces approximately over the center of each through lane, placing all primary faces on the far side of the intersection, considering supplemental signal faces for traffic behind large vehicles, and providing backplates on all signal faces. Citations: - Section 4D.06 Guidance §5 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §6 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §17 (p.671) - Section 4D.05 Guidance §7 (p.668) Note on sufficiency: The evidence establishes the framework (Table 4D-2 minimum sight distance) and the recommended treatment (warning sign), but the exact minimum sight distance in feet for a 45 mph 85th-percentile speed is not contained in the provided excerpt of Table 4D-2.

**Gold answer:** They should be continuously visible from at least 460 feet in advance of the stop line. If at least two faces are not continuously visible for that distance, a warning sign should be installed for approaching traffic.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Figure 4I-4, Figure 4I-3, p.724, p.725, Page 765. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. | The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.927 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Section 4I.06 and Figure 4I-4, the pedestrian intervals occur in this order: a Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, 7 seconds minimum, reducible under some conditions), followed immediately by a Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, with an optional countdown display), followed by a Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) of at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released. The Buffer Interval must always be provided and displayed. The pedestrian clearance time is normally timed using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, with a slower speed considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Standards (mandatory): - Per the Figure 4I-4 note: "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." - The full-page views of the Section 4I.06 Standard text further show that the buffer interval, consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK), shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement, and that the sum of the pedestrian change interval and buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time [Image 6]. Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - "Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time." Options (permitted): - "The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval." - Per the Figure 4I-4 notes: the countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less, and the Buffer Interval may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time or may begin after the calculated clearance time has ended. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence — steady UPRAISED HAND, Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, "7 seconds MIN."), Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown), and Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND, "2 seconds MIN.") — along with the "zero" point of the countdown display and five possible relationships between the buffer interval and vehicular yellow/red clearance intervals. - [Image 5] The full page containing Figure 4I-4 also shows the Guidance text specifying the 3.5 feet per second walking speed and the note that the Buffer Interval shall always be provided and displayed. - [Image 6] The full page containing Figure 4I-3 shows the Standard that the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND) shall begin immediately after the WALKING PERSON indication, followed by a buffer interval displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 765 - Page 764

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.733 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A pedestrian hybrid beacon rests dark between actuations. After a pedestrian actuation, its sequence is: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) both steady red indications, (5) alternating flashing red, then (6) dark again until the next actuation. The pedestrian walk interval corresponds to stage 4 — the display of both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3: "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six-stage sequence — 1. Dark Until Activated, 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation, 3. Steady Yellow, 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval (both red sections steady, labeled SR), 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval (labeled FR), and 6. Dark Again Until Activated — confirming that stage 4 is the pedestrian walk interval. [Image 5] shows the same Figure 4J-3 in the full-page context of pages containing Sections 4J.02–4J.03, including the Standard text of Section 4J.03. [Image 3] Figure 4I-4 illustrates the corresponding pedestrian signal displays (steady WALKING PERSON during the walk interval, flashing UPRAISED HAND during the change interval) that operate alongside the beacon. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.02 Guidance §4 (p.729) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 770

**Gold answer:** Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval.

## M017

**Question:** Using the definition of ADS and Table 5A-1, explain why Level 2 is not an ADS level while Level 3 is.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Section 1C.03, Section 5A.02, Table 5A-1, p.760, p.31. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. | Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD defines an Automated Driving System (ADS) as hardware and software capable of performing the *entire* Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, and states the term applies specifically to Level 3, 4, or 5 systems. In Table 5A-1, a Level 2 system executes only steering and acceleration/deceleration while "the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task," so it does not perform the entire DDT and is categorized as ADAS. A Level 3 system performs "all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD" (with a human expected to respond to intervention requests), which meets the ADS definition, so Table 5A-1 groups Levels 3–5 under the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" category. Standards (mandatory): - "The following acronyms and abbreviations, when used in this Manual, shall have the following meanings: … ADS-Automated Driving System …" (This establishes the mandatory meaning of the acronym; the substantive definition itself is Support.) Guidance (recommended): (none) Options (permitted): (none) Support/Definitions (informational): - "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." - Table 5A-1 describes Level 2 as "the driving mode specific execution by one or more sustained driver assistance systems of both steering and acceleration/deceleration … with the expectation that the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task," placing it in the "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" category. - Table 5A-1 describes Level 3 as "the driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," placing it in the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" category. - For background, driving automation system technology "automates some or all aspects of the driving tasks to assist or replace the human driver and can include driver assistance technology generally known as advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS)." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, in which the "Automation Category" column groups Levels 1–2 under "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" and Levels 3–5 under "Automated Driving System (ADS)." [Image 5] shows the full page containing Table 5A-1 together with the Level 2 and Level 3 descriptions confirming that only Levels 3 and above involve an ADS performing all aspects of the DDT. [Image 7] shows the Chapter 5A definitions page, including the ADS definition tying the term specifically to Level 3, 4, or 5 systems. Citations: - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31) - Section 5A.02 Support §1 (p.759)

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the order encountered by traffic, a TTC zone is typically divided into four areas: the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area. The activity area — the section of highway where the work actually takes place — is itself comprised of three components: the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space. This information is explanatory (Support) rather than a mandatory requirement. Visual evidence: [Image 1] (Figure 6B-1) shows the four areas in travel order from bottom (upstream) to top (downstream) — the advance warning area ("tells traffic what to expect ahead"), the transition area ("moves traffic out of its normal path" via a taper of channelizing devices), the activity area ("is where work takes place") containing the hatched work space, the traffic space allowing traffic to pass through, and both lateral and longitudinal buffer spaces, and finally the termination area ("lets traffic resume normal operations") with a downstream taper. Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772)

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Because 35 mph is "40 mph or less," Table 6B-4 gives L = WS²/60 = (12 × 35²)/60 = (12 × 1,225)/60 = **245 feet**. Using Table 6B-3, the minimum taper lengths are: merging taper at least L = **245 ft**; shifting taper at least 0.5 L = **122.5 ft (≈123 ft)**; and shoulder taper at least 0.33 L ≈ **81 ft (≈82 ft)**. Guidance (recommended): - "The appropriate taper length (L) should be determined using the criteria shown in Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4." - "A shifting taper should have a length of approximately ½ L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4)." - "If used, shoulder tapers should have a length of approximately ¹⁄3 L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4). If a shoulder is used as a travel lane, either through practice or during a TTC activity, a normal merging or shifting taper should be used." - For speeds less than 45 mph, "the formula L = WS2/60 should be used to compute the taper length," where W is the width of the offset in feet and S is the speed in mph. Support (explanatory): - "Tapers are created by using a series of channelizing devices and/or pavement markings to move traffic out of or into the normal path. Types of tapers are shown in Figure 6B-2." Table 6B-3 lists: Merging Taper — at least L; Shifting Taper — at least 0.5 L. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 6B-2 illustrates the merging taper at length L, the shifting tapers at ½ L, and the shoulder taper at ⅓ L, with a note to see Table 6B-4 for calculating L. - [Image 2] Table 6B-4 shows that for speeds of "40 mph or less," L = WS²/60, where L = taper length in feet, W = width of offset in feet, and S = speed in mph. - [Image 3] Table 6B-3 lists taper length criteria: Merging Taper "at least L," Shifting Taper "at least 0.5 L," Shoulder Taper "at least 0.33 L," with a note to use Table 6B-4 to calculate L. - [Image 5] The full page view of Figure 6B-2 confirms the L, ½ L, and ⅓ L relationships for merging, shifting, and shoulder tapers respectively. Citations: - Section 6B.08 Guidance §4 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §8 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §10 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Support §2 (p.775) - Section 3B.13 Guidance §2 (p.568) - Table 6B-4 (p.775) - Table 6B-3 (p.775) - Figure 6B-2 (p.774)

**Gold answer:** For 40 mph or less, L = W*S²/60 = 12*35²/60 = 245 feet. Minimum merging taper: 245 feet; shifting taper: 122.5 feet; shoulder taper: about 80.9 feet (0.33L, commonly rounded appropriately in design).

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device for flaggers, with flags reserved for emergency situations only. To stop traffic, the flagger must face road users, aim the STOP face toward them in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally, and hold the free arm with the palm above shoulder level toward approaching traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "To stop road users, the flagger shall face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward road users in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally away from the body. The free arm shall be held with the palm of the hand above shoulder level toward approaching traffic." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." Support (informational): - The optimum method of displaying the STOP or SLOW message is to place the paddle on a rigid staff tall enough that, with the staff end resting on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6D-1 labels the STOP/SLOW paddle as the "PREFERRED METHOD" (with the flag marked "EMERGENCY SITUATIONS ONLY") and shows the stop-traffic posture: R1-1 STOP paddle (18 inches minimum) held on a staff with the free hand raised, palm toward traffic. [Image 2] Table 6G-1 lists the STOP face on the STOP/SLOW paddle (R1-1, Section 6D.02) at 18 x 18 inches. Citations: - Section 6D.02 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6D.05 Standard §3 (p.785) - Section 6D.02 Support §5 (p.783) - Figure 6D-1 (p.784) - Table 6G-1 (p.793)

**Gold answer:** The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 6L-3, a Type C arrow board must have a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, a minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and a minimum of 15 elements. In addition, every arrow board (including Type C) must provide three categories of mode selections: (A) a Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; (B) a flashing Double Arrow mode; and (C) a flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode. Standards (mandatory): - "Arrow boards shall meet the minimum size, legibility distance, number of elements, and other specifications shown in Figure 6L-3." For Type C, Figure 6L-3 specifies 96 x 48 inches minimum size, 1 mile minimum legibility distance, and 15 minimum elements. - "An arrow board shall have the following three mode selections: A. A Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; B. A flashing Double Arrow mode; and C. A flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode." - "The arrow board shall have suitable elements capable of the various operating modes. The color presented by the elements shall be yellow." - "An arrow board shall be a sign with a matrix of elements capable of either flashing or sequential displays." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 6L-3 tabulates Type C as 96 x 48 inches minimum size, 1 mile minimum legibility distance, and 15 minimum elements, and illustrates the required display modes (Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, Sequential Chevron, Flashing Double Arrow, Flashing Caution, and Alternating Diamond Caution) using a Type C arrow board. - [Image 5] Page 875 shows the full Figure 6L-3 page, confirming the Type C row (96 x 48 inches, 1 mile, 15 elements) and the three numbered operating-mode categories. - [Image 6] Page 874 shows the Section 6L.06 Standard text requiring conformance with Figure 6L-3 and listing the three required mode selections. Support (informational): Type C arrow boards are intended to be used on high-speed, high-volume motor vehicle traffic control projects. Citations: - Section 6L.06 Standard §5 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §16 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §13 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §1 (p.832) - Section 6L.06 Support §6 (p.833) - Figure 6L-3 (p.834) - Page 875 - Page 874

**Gold answer:** Minimum size 96 x 48 inches, minimum legibility distance 1 mile, and at least 15 elements. It must provide at least one directional merge mode, the flashing double-arrow mode, and at least one caution mode.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT | Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. | S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted. Adjudication note: Correct advance sign, zone start, and normal S5-3 termination; omits the S5-2 same-endpoint alternative in the direct answer. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.33.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On approach to a reduced school speed limit zone, drivers must first encounter a School (S1-1) sign installed in advance of the speed limit signing, then the School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign placed at (or as near as practicable to) the point where the reduced zone begins. Where the speed reduction exceeds 10 mph, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5/S4-5a) sign is recommended in advance, and if used it must be followed by the School Speed Limit signing. Leaving the zone, the downstream end must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - Where higher fines apply only to speeding: "a FINES HIGHER (R2-6P), FINES DOUBLE (R2-6aP), or $XX FINE (R2-6bP) plaque shall be posted with the School Speed Limit (S5-1) sign and shall not be posted beneath the School Zone (S1-1) sign (see Section 7B.05)." Guidance (recommended): - "A Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign (see Figure 7B-1) should be used to inform road users of a reduced speed zone where the speed limit is being reduced by more than 10 mph, or where engineering judgment indicates that advance notice would be appropriate." Visual evidence: [Image 3] Figure 7B-2 shows the full sequence in each direction — an advance School (S1-1) sign with optional SCHOOL (S4-3P) plaque, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 SPEED LIMIT 20, and S4-1P time-of-day plaque) at the beginning of the zone, and an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (or END SCHOOL ZONE S5-2) with an optional SPEED LIMIT 45 (R2-1) sign at the downstream end. [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 depicts the individual signs and assemblies used in the sequence, including the School Speed Limit Assembly, S5-1 When Flashing sign, S4-5/S4-5a advance signs, and the S5-2/S5-3 end signs. [Image 1] Table 2C-3 provides the advance placement distances used to locate the advance School (S1-1) sign relative to the speed limit assembly. [Image 4] Table 7B-1 lists the sizes of the S1-1, S4-5/S4-5a, S5-1, S5-2, S5-3, and associated plaques in the sequence. [Image 5] Page 1019 contains the Section 7B.05 Standard text establishing this sequence. [Image 7] Page 1016 shows Figure 7B-2 in full-page context. [Image 8] Page 1020 shows a companion example (higher fines only for speeding) with the same entering/leaving sequence plus the R2-6 series plaque on the School Speed Limit assembly. [Image 6] Page 1024 contains the Section 7B.06 provisions on fines plaques associated with the School Speed Limit sign. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.06 Standard §3 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Guidance §13 (p.980) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Table 7B-1 (p.971) - Page 1019 - Page 1016 - Page 1020 - Page 1024

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.
