**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At a passive grade crossing with two or more tracks, the assembly is arranged top-to-bottom on a single support as: the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top (mounted at approximately 9 feet), the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque immediately below the Crossbuck, and the YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign below the plaque, with a retroreflective strip extending down the support to within 2 feet of the roadway. The Number of Tracks plaque is required whenever two or more tracks are present at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "If a Crossbuck sign support at a passive grade crossing does not include a YIELD or STOP sign..., a vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used for the full length of the front of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway." - Per the notes in Figure 8B-2: "Mounting height shall be at least 4 feet for installations of YIELD or STOP signs on existing Crossbuck sign supports" and "at least 5 feet for new installations in rural areas and at least 7 feet for new installations in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely." Guidance (recommended): - The use of STOP signs at passive grade crossings should be limited to unusual conditions where a Diagnostic Team determines a full stop is necessary, considering line of sight, number of tracks, train and vehicle speeds, and crash history. - If the YIELD or STOP sign is installed on a separate support, it should be placed in the same plane as the Crossbuck sign and closer to the traveled way, with a minimum 2-inch separation between the nearest points of the two signs. Options (permitted): - A strip of retroreflective white material not less than 2 inches in width may be used on the back of each blade of each Crossbuck sign at active grade crossings. Visual evidence: [Image 2] (Figure 8B-2) shows the vertical order — the Crossbuck at approximately 9 feet, the "3 TRACKS" inverted-T plaque directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign beneath, with the retroreflective strip extending down the support to within 2 feet of the roadway edge. [Image 1] (Figure 8B-1) shows the sign designs, including the R15-1 Crossbuck and the R15-2P Number of Tracks plaque with its inverted-T shape. [Image 6] shows a comparable Crossbuck Assembly example with a "2 TRACKS" plaque below the Crossbuck and a YIELD or STOP sign below that at a pathway/sidewalk grade crossing. Citations: - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §20 (p.1000) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §6 (p.997) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §13 (p.1000) - Section 8B.03 Option §9 (p.995) - Figure 8B-2 (p.993) - Figure 8B-1 (p.996) - Page 1080

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box, if used, must be at least 10 feet long (measured from its upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge nearest the crosswalk or intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Turns on red are prohibited from any lane where a bicycle box is placed. Green-colored pavement is optional in a bicycle box, but if used it must cover the full limits of the box, and green pavement generally is limited to specific bicycle applications, including bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." (Section 9E.12 Standard, shown on the full page view of the section text.) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." (Section 9E.12 Standard, shown on the page containing Figure 9E-12, Sheet 1 of 2.) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes …, B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections …, C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane …, D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes …, E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols …" Options (permitted): - A bicycle box may be used to increase the visibility of stopped bicycles on the approach to a signalized intersection during the red portion of the signal cycle for the approach lane(s) behind the box. - "Green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1 of 2) shows a bicycle box across one lane with a "10 ft MIN." dimension between the intersection stop line and the advance stop line, a bicycle symbol inside the box, and green-colored pavement labeled "(optional)." [Image 6] The full page view of Section 9E.12 shows the Standard text including the 10-foot minimum, the bicycle symbol requirement, and "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." [Image 7] The full page with Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1) shows the Standard "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." [Image 5] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 2 of 2) shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with the "10 ft MIN." dimension and the required NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign, consistent with the turn-on-red prohibition. [Image 4] Figure 9E-11's page also displays the Section 9E.12 Standard provisions, including the turn-on-red prohibition. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Option §1 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Option §12 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Figure 9E-12 (p.1106) - Figure 9E-11 (p.1105) - Page 1146 - Page 1147 - Page 1148

**Gold answer:** The bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long, include at least one bicycle symbol, prohibit turns on red from its lane, and, if green pavement is used, fill the entire limits of the box.

## T001

**Question:** What is the purpose of the MUTCD, and what four objectives are used to achieve that purpose?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 U.S.C. 109(d). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility | promote consistency in use, installation, and operation | The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. This purpose is achieved through four objectives: (A) promote safety, inclusion, and mobility for all users of the road network; (B) promote efficiency through creating national uniformity in the meaning and appearance of traffic control devices; (C) promote national consistency in the use, installation, and operation of traffic control devices; and (D) provide basic principles for traffic engineers to use in making decisions regarding the use, installation, operation, maintenance, and removal of traffic control devices. Note that the purpose and objectives are Support (explanatory) material, though the MUTCD itself carries mandatory legal status as the national standard. Standards (mandatory): - "The Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices for Streets and Highways (MUTCD) is incorporated by reference in 23 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 655, Subpart F and shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a)." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility; promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promote consistency in use, installation, and operation; and provide basic decision-making principles for traffic engineers.

## T002

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 CFR 655.603(a). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.877 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD's applicability to a facility open to public travel does not depend on whether the facility is publicly or privately owned, nor on its funding source. The controlling factor is whether the facility is open to public travel; the Manual applies to both public and private streets, roadways, bikeways, and toll roads open to the public, and it explicitly does not apply to certain facilities (such as gated private roadways restricted at all times) regardless of ownership. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: A. Any street, roadway, or bikeway open to public travel, either publicly or privately owned; B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions... C. Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) Note: Section 1A.01 Support §5 states this principle directly — "The applicability of the MUTCD to facilities open to public travel is independent of the type of ownership or jurisdiction (public or private) and the source of funding (Federal, State, local, or private)" — but as a Support provision it is explanatory rather than normative; the binding requirements are the Standard provisions quoted above.

**Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

## T003

**Question:** Are islands, curbs, speed humps, raised roadway surfaces, and rumble strips considered traffic control devices under the MUTCD?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.02, Section 3I.01, Section 6A.03, p.768, p.1, p.632. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.920 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that infrastructure elements restricting travel paths or vehicle speeds—such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces—are not traffic control devices, and that transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are likewise not traffic control devices. This is consistent with the definition of traffic control devices, which is limited to signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, and other devices communicating regulatory, warning, or guidance messages. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices shall be defined as all signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices that use colors, shapes, symbols, words, sounds, or tactile information for the primary purpose of communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users on a street, highway, pedestrian facility, bikeway, pathway, or site roadway open to public travel." Islands, curbs, speed humps, raised surfaces, and rumble strips do not fall within this definition. Support (informational context, not normative): - Section 1A.02 explains: "Infrastructure elements that restrict the road user's travel paths or vehicle speeds, such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces, are not traffic control devices. Transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are also not traffic control devices." It further notes that operational devices such as fencing, roadway lighting, barriers, and attenuators are shown in the Manual for context only. - Section 3I.01 confirms that "Although they are not considered to be traffic control devices, raised islands (see Chapter 3J) are also sometimes used to channelize traffic" — meaning they can serve channelizing functions while still not being traffic control devices. Citations: - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) - Section 1A.02 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 3I.01 Support §2 (p.632)

**Gold answer:** No. Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips, are not traffic control devices.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Adjudication note: Correct Standard, Guidance, and Option distinctions; the full Support definition is not stated. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD defines its four text headings in Section 1C.01. A Standard is a required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive statement (typically using "shall"); Guidance is a recommended practice from which deviations are allowed based on engineering judgment or study (typically using "should"); and an Option is a permissive condition carrying no requirement or recommendation, sometimes modifying a Standard or Guidance statement. The evidence provided is cut off before the full definition of Support and before stating the verb typically used in Option statements, so I can only note that Support is explanatory/informational (e.g., typical-application information not labeled as a note is generally regarded as Guidance, per Section 6P.01). Standards (mandatory): - "When used in this Manual, the text headings of Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support shall be defined as follows..." - "Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. ... All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Option-a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement. All Option statements are labeled..." - "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Guidance (recommended): (none in evidence) Options (permitted): (none in evidence) Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857) Note on evidence limits: The excerpt of Section 1C.01 is truncated before the definition of the Support heading and before any statement of the verb typically associated with Option statements (commonly "may"). The evidence is therefore insufficient to quote the MUTCD's verbatim definition of Support or the Option verb convention.

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

Note that the formal definitions of "engineering study" and "engineering judgment" reside in Section 1C.02, which is not included in the evidence, so the precise definitional wording cannot be quoted here. Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." - For Standard statements: "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." For Guidance statements: deviations are "allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate." **Gold answer:** Both involve evaluating pertinent information and applying engineering principles by a professional engineer or a person under such an engineer's supervision. Engineering judgment does not require documentation; an engineering study must be documented in writing.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The general rule is that a non-compliant traffic control device being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable must be replaced with a compliant device. The limited exception permits replacement in kind, based on engineering judgment, when a single compliant device within a series of non-compliant devices would confuse road users and/or the replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely MUTCD compliance. Standards (mandatory): - "A non-compliant traffic control device that is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) for any reason shall be replaced with a compliant device, except as provided for in Paragraph 13 of this Section." - "Unless a particular device is no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02), non-compliant devices on existing highways and bikeways shall be brought into compliance with the current edition of the National MUTCD as part of the systematic upgrading of substandard traffic control devices (and installation of new required traffic control devices) required pursuant to the Highway Safety Program, 23 U.S.C. §402(a)." - "Channelizing devices that are no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) shall be replaced." Guidance (recommended): - Agencies should establish maintenance policies that incorporate effective practices to identify and then fix or replace in a timely manner any traffic control device that is reaching the end of its useful life, or that is damaged or otherwise no longer serviceable. - Functional maintenance of traffic control devices should be used to determine if certain devices need to be changed to meet current traffic conditions. Options (permitted): - A non-compliant traffic control device may be replaced in kind when engineering judgment indicates it is more appropriate because: (A) one compliant device in the midst of a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would be confusing to road users, and/or (B) the schedule for replacement of the whole series of non-compliant devices will result in achieving timely compliance with the MUTCD. Citations: - Section 1B.03 Standard §12 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Option §13 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §5 (p.5) - Section 6K.01 Standard §15 (p.815) - Section 5A.04 Guidance §6 (p.762) - Section 1D.10 Guidance §1 (p.38)

**Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

**Gold answer:** No. A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The experimenting agency must submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If required semi-annual progress reports are missed twice in a row, the experiment is terminated and FHWA's approval is rescinded, requiring the site(s) to be restored to MUTCD-compliant condition within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A new traffic control device is not eligible for official experimentation or interim approval unless it is in the public domain, meaning all forms of proprietary protection (patents, trademarks, copyrights) have been expressly abandoned. For experimentation, the agency, manufacturer/developer, and supplier must all provide a legally-binding certification of public-domain status, acknowledging that any future proprietary protection will result in removal from the MUTCD or cancellation of the approval or authorization. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - A request for experimentation involving a new device or new application "shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation." - "The legally-binding statement shall also state that the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device are aware that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other items, "a legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

These are Guidance (recommended) provisions, and the MUTCD advises considering design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity to maximize consistency with these principles. Guidance (recommended): - To be effective, a traffic control device should: A. Fulfill a need; B. Command attention; C. Convey a clear, simple meaning; D. Command respect from road users; and E. Give adequate time for proper response. - Design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity should be carefully considered to maximize a device's ability to be consistent with the five principles; vehicle speed and road-user types should be carefully considered as elements governing design, operation, placement, and location. - The proper use of traffic control devices should provide the road user with the information necessary to safely, efficiently, and lawfully use the streets, highways, pedestrian facilities, and bikeways. - Placement of a device should be within the road user's view for adequate visibility, appropriately positioned with respect to the location, object, or situation to which it applies, and located and legible so a road user has adequate time to respond in both day and night conditions. - Devices should be placed and operated in a uniform and consistent manner as part of maintaining uniformity in traffic control. - When developing messages for display on changeable message signs (CMS), Section 1D.01 should be consulted for the principles of an effective traffic control device. Citations: - Section 1D.01 Guidance §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §4 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §5 (p.31) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §3 (p.38) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §4 (p.38) - Section 2L.04 Guidance §3 (p.513)

**Gold answer:** It should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response.

Purple-restricted to use only by vehicles with registered electronic toll collection (ETC) accounts ..." - "The standard colors of red, orange, yellow, purple, or the fluorescent versions thereof, fluorescent yellow-green, and fluorescent pink shall not be used as background colors for community wayfinding guide signs, in order to minimize possible confusion with critical, higher-priority regulatory and warning sign color meanings readily understood by road users." - "The standard colors of red, orange, yellow, purple, or the fluorescent versions thereof, fluorescent yellow-green, and fluorescent pink shall not be used as background colors for Destination Guide signs for shared-use paths, in order to minimize possible confusion with critical, higher-priority regulatory and warning sign color meanings readily understood by path users." Support (informational): - The fluorescent version of orange provides higher conspicuity than standard orange, especially during twilight. Citations: - Section 1D.05 Standard §2 (p.33) - Section 2D.55 Standard §19 (p.271) - Section 9D.12 Standard §27 (p.1084) - Section 6F.01 Support §3 (p.789)

**Gold answer:** Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts.

**Gold answer:** No. They may not bear advertising or any message unrelated to traffic control. Acknowledgment signs, Specific Service signs, and Tourist-Oriented Directional signs are not treated as advertising under this provision.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs by function into three principal categories: regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations; warning signs give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent; and guide signs provide route, destination, direction, distance, service, and similar geographical, recreational, or cultural information. This functional classification is a mandatory (Standard) definition, and it carries through to design rules when messages of one functional type are incorporated into signs of another type. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." Guidance (recommended): - Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except where one sign supplements another (among other listed exceptions), reflecting that signs of different functions are generally displayed separately. Support (informational): - A changeable message sign (CMS) is a traffic control device at all times, and the design, format, and display limitations of conventional signs apply to CMS regulatory, warning, and guidance messages alike. - Examples of regulatory and warning signs used together at roundabouts and neighborhood traffic circles are shown in Figures 2B-21 through 2B-24, with guide signs for roundabouts addressed separately in Section 2D.39. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows examples of heights and lateral placements for signs of different functional types, including a regulatory STOP sign, a warning sign with advisory speed plaque, route assemblies, and guide signs. [Image 2] shows placement examples of typical regulatory signs (STOP, YIELD) at various intersection configurations. [Image 3] relates the relative locations of signs addressed in Chapters 2B (regulatory), 2C (warning), and other chapters. [Image 4] provides advance placement guidelines specific to warning signs based on posted speed and deceleration conditions. [Image 5] is a table-of-contents page listing Chapter 2C "Warning Signs and Object Markers" sections, illustrating how warning signs form a distinct functional chapter. [Image 6] is a table-of-contents page for temporary traffic control zone regulatory (Chapter 6G) and warning (Chapter 6H) sign chapters, showing the functional split carried into Part 6. [Image 7] shows Table 2A-2, Common Uses of Sign Colors, which distinguishes color schemes by functional type (Regulatory, Warning, Guide, etc.). [Image 8] shows Figure 2B-23, an example of regulatory and warning signs applied together at a two-lane roundabout. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2L.01 Support §3 (p.510) - Section 2B.51 Support §6 (p.123) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Figure 2A-4 (p.50) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 23 - Page 9 - Page 85 - Page 162

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In general, all word messages on signs must be aligned horizontally and read left to right, and must use the standard wording shown in the Manual and the "Standard Highway Signs" publication. Casing rules vary by sign type: for example, on community wayfinding guide signs, destination lettering must be mixed-case (initial capitals with lower-case letters) while all other word messages must be all upper-case. Minor modifications to the layout of word messages are allowed, but shapes and colors must remain as standardized. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05)." - "The lettering for destinations on community wayfinding guide signs shall be a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (see Section 2D.04). All other word messages on community wayfinding guide signs shall be in all upper-case letters." - "Standardization of these designs does not preclude further improvement by minor modifications to the orientation of symbols (see Section 2A.09), width of borders, or layout of word messages, but all shapes and colors shall be as indicated." Guidance (recommended): - For pavement marking word messages of more than one line, the message should read in the direction of travel, with the first word nearest to the road user. - Word messages (on community wayfinding signs) should be as brief as practical, with lettering large enough to provide the necessary legibility distance. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows example CMS messages composed entirely in upper-case lettering with horizontally aligned lines, consistent with general word-message orientation practice (with a note that certain full-matrix CMS should use upper- and lower-case lettering where appropriate). [Image 2] illustrates Destination Guide signing on a shared-use path, where destination lettering is required to be mixed-case (initial upper-case with lower-case letters) while all other word messages are all upper-case — paralleling the community wayfinding casing rule. [Image 3] shows standard abbreviations used when full word messages cannot be accommodated on portable changeable message signs. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.55 Standard §32 (p.271) - Section 2A.04 Standard §8 (p.40) - Section 3B.21 Guidance §2 (p.575) - Section 2D.55 Guidance §28 (p.271) - Table 2L-3 (p.517) - Figure 9D-5 (p.1082) - Table 1D-2 (p.37)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A NEW plaque should remain in place for a period determined by engineering judgment, but no longer than 12 months after installation. Strobe lights are prohibited as a means of enhancing sign conspicuity, although other conspicuity-enhancement methods (such as larger signs, dual signing, header panels, or the NEW plaque itself) are permitted. Standards (mandatory): - "Strobe lights shall not be used to enhance the conspicuity of highway signs." Guidance (recommended): - If a NEW plaque is used, it should remain in place for a period of time determined by engineering judgment, but not more than 12 months. - The NEW plaque should be removed no later than 12 months after it was installed. Options (permitted): - Based on engineering judgment, conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign may be enhanced by methods including increasing sign size, dual signing on the left-hand side, adding a solid yellow or fluorescent yellow header panel, or adding a NEW plaque above a new standard regulatory or warning sign for a period of time in accordance with the Guidance above. - A NEW (W16-15P) plaque may be mounted above a regulatory sign when a new regulation takes effect, or above an advance warning sign (such as a Signal Ahead sign for a newly-installed traffic control signal) to warn of a new traffic condition. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §4 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Guidance §3 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Option §1 (p.197) - Section 2C.60 Guidance §2 (p.197)

**Gold answer:** The NEW plaque should remain only for a period determined by engineering judgment and no more than 12 months. Strobe lights may not be used to enhance highway-sign conspicuity.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In rural areas, roadside signs must be mounted with a minimum of 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (or where the sign's view might be obstructed), the minimum is 7 feet, measured from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot rural / 7-foot urban minimums apply to temporary traffic control signs and to YIELD or STOP signs at crossbuck assemblies. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For grade crossings: "If a Crossbuck Assembly is installed on a new sign support (see Figure 8B-2) or if the YIELD or STOP sign is installed on a separate support (see Figure 8B-3), the mounting height... shall be at least 5 feet in rural areas and shall be at least 7 feet in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." Guidance (recommended): - Except as otherwise provided, signs mounted on portable sign supports that do not meet the minimum mounting heights provided in Part 2 should not be used for a duration of more than 3 days. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 shows a roadside sign in a rural area with a "5 ft MIN." height (panel A) and a roadside sign in a business, commercial, or residential area with a "7 ft* MIN." height, noted as applying "*Where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur" (panel C). - [Image 2] Figure 6F-1 shows temporary traffic control signs with a 5 ft MIN. height in rural areas (panel A) and 7 ft MIN. in business, commercial, or residential areas both with and without curb (panels C and D). - [Image 4] Figure 8B-3 shows a YIELD sign on a separate support with "5 ft MIN." in a rural area and "7 ft MIN." in an area with pedestrian movements or parking. - [Image 5] Page 831 (Figure 6F-1 with accompanying text) confirms the 5 ft rural minimum and the 7 ft minimum for business, commercial, or residential areas. - [Image 6] Page 92 (Section 2A.15 Mounting Height) contains the Standard text establishing the 5-foot rural and 7-foot business/commercial/residential minimums. - [Image 7] Page 99 (Figure 2A-2) illustrates typical installations with the 5 ft and 7 ft minimum mounting heights and lateral offsets. - [Image 8] Page 566 (Figure 2M-3) shows recreational/cultural interest area sign assemblies with 5 ft MIN. heights in rural areas and 7 ft MIN. in business, commercial, or residential areas. - [Image 3] Figure 8B-2 relates to Crossbuck Assembly installation referenced by the grade-crossing mounting height Standard. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 8B.04 Standard §12 (p.1000) - Section 6F.02 Guidance §11 (p.791) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 6F-1 (p.790) - Figure 8B-2 (p.993) - Figure 8B-3 (p.998) - Page 831 - Page 92 - Page 99 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2A.16, Section 2G.03, Section 2G.08, Section 2G.10, Figure 2A-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 3, which produced MRR=0.333. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.525 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, unless nearby structures have a lesser vertical clearance. This same 17-foot clearance applies to median-barrier post-mounted signs that overhang the traveled way, and to preferential lane signs mounted higher on median barriers where lateral clearance is limited. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." (Section 2A.15, shown in [Image 5]) - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." (Section 2A.15, shown in [Image 5]) - Related support-placement requirement: "For overhead sign supports, the minimum lateral offset from the edge of the shoulder (or if no shoulder exists, from the edge of the pavement) to the near edge of overhead sign supports (cantilever or sign bridges) shall be 6 feet. Overhead sign supports shall have a barrier or crash cushion to shield them if they are within the clear zone." Options (permitted): - If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures in order to improve visibility of the overhead signs (shown in [Image 5]). - In special cases the clearance to overhead signs may be reduced if necessary because of substandard dimensions in tunnels and other major structures such as double-deck bridges (shown in [Image 5]). - Where lateral clearance is limited, Preferential Lane regulatory signs post-mounted on a median barrier that are 72 inches or less in width may be mounted higher, such that the vertical clearance to the bottom of the sign, light fixture, or structural support, whichever is lowest, is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders. - The same 17-foot minimum applies where warning signs on median barriers for preferential lanes are mounted higher due to limited lateral clearance. - The same 17-foot minimum applies where Preferential Lane guide signs 72 inches or less in width are mounted higher due to limited lateral clearance. Visual evidence: - [Image 3] Figure 2A-2 (panel H, "Overhead sign") illustrates the 17 ft MIN. vertical clearance from the pavement to an overhead-mounted sign, along with the 6 ft MIN. lateral offset to the support. - [Image 5] shows the Section 2A.15 Standard text requiring not less than 17 feet of vertical clearance for overhead signs over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, plus the Options allowing reduced clearance near lower structures or in tunnels. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Standard §1 (p.59) - Section 2G.03 Option §14 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Option §4 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Option §18 (p.422) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 100

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

Guidance (recommended): - At toll plazas, regulatory signs or regulatory panels indicating restrictions on vehicle type and forms of toll payment should be installed over the applicable lane, either on the toll plaza canopy or on a separate structure immediately in advance of the canopy, located so each sign is clearly related to an individual toll lane. Options (permitted): - Where ramp control signals meter traffic on a freeway or expressway entrance ramp, regulatory signs with legends appropriate to the control may be installed adjacent to the ramp control signal faces. Guidance (recommended): - The TRAFFIC FROM LEFT (RIGHT) DOES NOT STOP (W4-4aP) plaque or ONCOMING TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4bP) plaque should be used at intersections where STOP signs control all but one approach to the intersection, unless the only non-stopped approach is from a one-way street. Options (permitted): - The CROSS TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4P) plaque may be used in combination with a STOP sign when engineering judgment indicates conditions are causing or could cause road users to misinterpret the intersection as an all-way stop. - The W4-4aP or W4-4bP plaque may be used when such messages more accurately describe the traffic controls established at the intersection. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the STOP (R1-1) sign and the ALL WAY (R1-3P) plaque with white legend on red background, along with other permissible STOP/YIELD plaques such as EXCEPT RIGHT TURN (R1-10P). **Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

Options (permitted): - YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts. - At roundabouts where ONE WAY signs have been installed in the central island, Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques may be placed below the YIELD signs on approaches to supplement the central island signs. - The Vehicular Traffic Warning sign assembly may be installed on an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. - The Non-Vehicular Warning sign assembly may likewise be installed on a YIELD-controlled approach where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to a circulatory roadway. Visual evidence: [Image 1] (Figure 3D-1) shows YIELD word pavement markings and yield-line triangles placed on each approach roadway (not on the circulatory roadway), with crosswalks dimensioned at "20 ft MIN." from the circulatory roadway edge. [Image 7] (Page 119) contains Section 2B.51 text permitting Roundabout Circulation plaques below YIELD signs on roundabout approaches. **Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

However, the evidence does show how they may be adjusted: when the 85th-percentile approach speed of the major-street traffic exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the Item A and Item B values. Options (permitted): - "If the 85th-percentile approach speed of the major-street traffic exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the values given in Items A and B in Paragraph 1 of this Section." The evidence is insufficient to state the two hourly volume thresholds themselves.

**Gold answer:** For the same 8 hours of a typical day, the combined entering volume must be at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor-street approaches. If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values.

The response identifies: Section 2B.21, Section 2C.59, Section 2G.26, Section 2C.28, Section 2C.12, Section 4S.04. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. | It should not be used for an advisory speed | an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A Speed Limit (R2-1) sign must display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study, and the displayed limits must be in multiples of 5 mph. A Speed Limit sign should not be used to show an advisory speed for a roadway condition; instead, an advisory speed plaque mounted below a warning sign is the appropriate device for that purpose. Standards (mandatory): - "The Speed Limit (R2-1) sign (see Figure 2B-3) shall display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study. The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - For variable installations: "The regulatory speed displayed on a variable Speed Limit sign shall comply with Paragraph 13 of" Section 2B.21 (i.e., the same display and 5-mph increment requirements apply). Guidance (recommended): - Per Section 2B.21 Guidance (visible on Page 89, ¶23): "An advisory speed plaque (see Section 2C.59) mounted below a warning sign should be used to warn road users of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. A Speed Limit sign should not be used for this purpose." Options (permitted): - "An Advisory Speed plaque (see Figure 2C-1 and Section 2C.59) may be used when the change in roadway condition requires a reduced speed." - "An Advisory Exit Speed or Advisory Ramp Speed sign may be used based on engineering judgment when the difference between the mainline roadway speed limit and the exit or ramp advisory speed in the vicinity of the departure is 10 mph or less." - "A Speed Limit Sign Beacon may be used with a fixed or variable Speed Limit sign," and a flashing Speed Limit Sign Beacon (with an appropriate accompanying sign) may be used to indicate that the displayed speed limit is in effect. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-3 shows the R2-1 Speed Limit sign ("SPEED LIMIT 50") and related signs and plaques, all displaying limits in multiples of 5 mph. [Image 2] Figure 2C-1 shows the Advisory Speed plaque (W13-1P, "35 MPH") and advisory exit/ramp speed signs used with warning signs — the devices used instead of the R2-1 for advisory speeds. [Image 3] Figure 2C-4 shows the Vehicle Speed Feedback sign and plaque, which display an approaching vehicle's speed in relation to a posted speed limit or advisory speed. [Image 4] Figure 2C-9 shows speed zone warning signs (W3-5 series) used in advance of speed zones. [Image 5] Page 197 shows Figure 2C-2 with W13-1P Advisory Speed plaques posted with horizontal alignment warning signs on a curve. [Image 8] Page 89 contains Section 2B.21 ¶13 (the "multiples of 5 mph" Standard) and ¶23 (the Guidance stating a Speed Limit sign should not be used for advisory speed purposes). Citations: - Section 2B.21 Standard §13 (p.85) - Section 2G.26 Standard §3 (p.457) - Section 2C.28 Option §2 (p.173) - Section 2C.12 Option §5 (p.160) - Section 4S.04 Option §3 (p.753) - Section 2C.13 Support §1 (p.166) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86) - Figure 2C-1 (p.153) - Figure 2C-4 (p.166) - Figure 2C-9 (p.179) - Page 197 - Page 89

**Gold answer:** It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. It should not be used for an advisory speed; an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The three types of signs used at an exit-ramp terminal to deter wrong-way entry are the DO NOT ENTER (R5-1) sign, the WRONG WAY (R5-1a) sign, and ONE WAY (R6-1/R6-2) signs. The DO NOT ENTER sign is mandatory at the intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad, and Figure 2B-15 illustrates the coordinated application of all three sign types (with WRONG WAY signs on the ramp itself and ONE WAY signs at the ramp/crossroad intersection). Note that the supplied text excerpts do not include the full Section 2B.48 provision itself; the requirement is evidenced by the Section 2B.46 Standard and the figures shown. Standards (mandatory): - "The DO NOT ENTER (R5-1) sign (see Figure 2B-13) shall be used at the following locations: … B. The intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad as specified in Section 2B.48 (see Figure 2B-15)" (visible on Page 150, Section 2B.46). Guidance (recommended): - "To minimize wrong-way movements onto freeway or expressway exit ramps, Street Name signs should not be used at the intersection of a freeway or expressway exit ramp with the crossroad to display the name of the freeway or expressway to traffic on the crossroad." Options (permitted): - A Directional assembly with a diagonal downward-pointing arrow (M6-2aP) plaque may be used at the far left-hand corner of an intersection with an entrance ramp "to indicate the immediate point of entry to the freeway or expressway and distinguish the entrance ramp from an adjoining exit ramp terminal at the same intersection" — a permitted measure that helps prevent confusion between entrance and exit ramps. Visual evidence: - [Image 4] Figure 2B-15 shows the exit-ramp termination treatment with DO NOT ENTER signs (mounted back-to-back facing the ramp), WRONG WAY signs along the exit ramp, and ONE WAY signs at the ramp/crossroad intersection, plus optional turn-prohibition signs and wrong-way arrow pavement markings. - [Image 5] The full-page view of Figure 2B-15 (p.112) confirms the same combination of DO NOT ENTER, WRONG WAY, and ONE WAY signing with pavement markings at the exit ramp termination. - [Image 6] Page 150 contains Section 2B.46, whose Standard mandates the DO NOT ENTER sign at "the intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad as specified in Section 2B.48," and Figure 2B-13 depicting the DO NOT ENTER (R5-1), WRONG WAY (R5-1a), and ONE WAY (R6-1, R6-2) signs. - [Image 7] The table of contents lists Figure 2B-15 "Example of Application of Regulatory Signing and Pavement Markings at an Exit Ramp Termination to Deter Wrong-Way Entry," confirming the figure's purpose. - [Image 2] Figure 2D-18 includes a note directing readers to "Section 2B.48 and Figure 2B-15 for an example of regulatory signing and pavement markings at an exit ramp termination to deter wrong-way entry." Citations: - Figure 2B-15 (p.112) - Page 150 - Page 153 - Page 29 - Section 2D.45 Guidance §3 (p.247) - Section 2D.50 Option §3 (p.254) - Figure 2D-18 (p.256)

**Gold answer:** At least one ONE WAY sign for each crossroad travel direction, at least one DO NOT ENTER sign near the downstream end of the exit ramp, and at least one WRONG WAY sign on the exit ramp facing wrong-way road users are required.

**Question:** How should the advance placement of warning signs be determined?

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6H.02, Section 6B.04, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1, p.798. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. | Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.501 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs is determined by providing adequate Perception-Response Time (PRT) based on speed and the condition being warned of, using Table 2C-3 as a guide applied with engineering judgment. For temporary traffic control (TTC) zones, signs should be placed at distances varying by roadway type, condition, and posted speed — roughly 4–8 times the speed limit (in feet) on urban streets, 8–12 times on rural highways, and ½ mile or more on freeways and expressways — with Table 6B-1 giving recommended spacing. Guidance (recommended): - Where highway conditions permit, warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed; where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign should be approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways (Table 6B-1). - On freeways and expressways, advance warning sign placement should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow, extending as far as ½ mile or more. - On urban streets, the effective placement of the nearest warning sign to the TTC zone, in feet, should range from 4 to 8 times the speed limit in mph, with the high end used when speeds are relatively high; the advance warning area should extend a greater distance when two or more signs are used on higher-speed streets. - On rural highways, the effective placement of the first warning sign in feet should be substantially longer — from 8 to 12 times the speed limit in mph — and the advance warning area should extend 1,500 feet or more for open highway conditions. - The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions; Table 2C-3 provides guidelines for advance placement distance based on posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A (speed reduction and lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to a listed advisory speed). - Per Table 2C-3 notes, distances are adjusted for sign legibility (180 feet for Condition A; 250 feet for Condition B), and for warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 6B-1 shows recommended warning sign minimum spacing (dimensions A, B, C) by road type, ranging from 100 feet on low-speed urban streets to 1,000/1,500/2,640 feet on expressways/freeways. - [Image 2] Table 2C-3 shows advance placement distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A and Condition B (e.g., 225 ft at 20 mph up to 1,600 ft at 85 mph for Condition A). - [Image 6] The full page view of Section 2C.04 shows the Guidance that warning signs should provide adequate PRT and that Table 2C-3 distances should be applied with engineering judgment. - [Image 8] The full page view of Chapter 6H (Section 6H.02) shows the Guidance on positioning advance warning signs in TTC zones with reference to Table 6B-1. Citations: - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §4 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §6 (p.771) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Table 6B-1 (p.773) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 839 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.

Their use must also conform to Table 2C-4, and spacing along the curve should follow Table 2C-5. Standards (mandatory): - "The Chevron Alignment sign shall be a vertical rectangle. No border shall be used on the Chevron Alignment sign." - "If used, Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed on the outside of a turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed at a minimum height of 4 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way." - "The use of the Chevron Alignment (W1-8) sign (see Figures 2C-1 and 2C-2) to provide additional emphasis and guidance for a change in horizontal alignment shall be in accordance with the information shown in Table 2C-4." Guidance (recommended): - "The Chevron Alignment signs should be visible for a sufficient distance to provide the road user with adequate time to react to the change in alignment." - "When the Hairpin Curve sign or the 270-degree Loop sign is installed, either a One-Direction Large Arrow (W1-6) sign or Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs should be installed on the outside of the turn or curve." Options (permitted): - "Chevron Alignment signs may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2C-4 (Charts A and B), which governs when devices such as chevrons are required — chevrons plus an advance horizontal alignment warning sign apply at speed differentials of 20 mph or more. [Image 2] shows Table 2C-5, giving typical chevron spacing by advisory speed and curve radius (e.g., 40 feet for 15 mph or less, up to 200 feet for more than 60 mph). [Image 5] shows the full Section 2C.08 page containing the shape, border, placement, orientation, and 4-foot minimum mounting height requirements alongside Table 2C-5. [Image 3] shows Figure 3G-1 delineator placement, with a note that Chevron Alignment signs (W1-8) may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators. **Gold answer:** They must be vertical rectangles with no border, installed on the outside of the turn or curve, aligned approximately at a right angle to approaching traffic, and mounted at least 4 feet above the near edge of the traveled way.

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2L.05, Table 2L-2, p.514, p.516, p.515. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. | It should contain no more than four words | total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.990 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A unit of information is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision (for example, "MAJOR CRASH" answers "What happened?"). Under MUTCD Guidance, a unit of information should contain no more than four words, and message length limits are expressed in terms of the number of units of information displayed. Guidance (recommended): - "A unit of information, which is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision, should not be more than four words." - The maximum length of a message should be dictated by the number of units of information in the message, in addition to the size of the CMS. - Related unit-of-information limits: no more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase; no more than four units of information should be in a message when traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more; no more than five units when speeds are less than 35 mph; and only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS. Options (permitted): - A unit of information consisting of more than one word may be displayed on more than one line, and an additional CMS at a downstream location may be used so the entire message can be read twice. Visual evidence: [Image 2] Table 2L-2 illustrates the concept with a four-unit example — "MAJOR CRASH" (what happened), "AT EXIT 12" (where), "TO NEW YORK" (who the advisory is for), and "USE ROUTE 46" (what is advised) — each counted as one unit of information and assembled into a two-phase message. Citations: - Section 2L.05 Guidance §1 (p.514) - Section 2L.05 Guidance §7 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §2 (p.515) - Section 2L.05 Option §9 (p.516) - Table 2L-2 (p.515)

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 9E.13, Figure 3B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.951 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The color of a longitudinal line communicates the directional relationship of the traffic it separates. Yellow center line markings separate traffic lanes moving in opposite directions of travel, while white lane line markings separate traffic lanes moving in the same direction of travel. The pattern of the yellow line (broken vs. solid) further communicates whether crossing for passing is permitted. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - "The center line markings on two-lane, two-way roadways shall be one of the following as shown in Figure 3B-1: A. Two-direction passing zone markings consisting of a normal width broken yellow line where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for traffic traveling in either direction; B. One-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of a double yellow line, one of which is a normal width broken yellow line and the other is a normal width solid yellow line, where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for the traffic traveling adjacent to the broken line, but is prohibited for traffic traveling adjacent to the solid line; or C. Two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of two normal width solid yellow lines where crossing the center line markings for passing is prohibited for traffic traveling in either direction." - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." Options (permitted): - On shared-use paths wide enough for two lanes, a solid yellow center line may be used to separate the two directions of travel where passing or traveling to the left of the line is not permitted, and a broken yellow center line may be used where passing is permitted. - Solid white lane line markings may be used: on approaches to intersections to separate contiguous through lanes; to separate through traffic lanes from auxiliary lanes, such as an added uphill truck lane; and on approaches to crosswalks across multi-lane roadways. **Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.

The response identifies: Section 3B.12, Section 3B.13, Section 8C.02, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.03, Section 2B.36.
