The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On roadways with center line markings, no-passing-zone markings are mandatory at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit; on two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways, no-passing zones must also be established at curves and other locations where an engineering study shows passing must be prohibited. When successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should be connected between the zones. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - "Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones." Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Crosswalk markings must be white, and transverse lines used to establish a crosswalk must be between 6 and 24 inches wide. High-visibility designs (longitudinal bar, ladder, bar pair) with their own dimensional ranges are permitted where added conspicuity is desired. At a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater, the minimum width of the crosswalk shall be 8 feet." [Image 5] Guidance (recommended): - Transverse line crosswalk markings should be limited to locations controlled by traffic control signals or on approaches controlled by STOP or YIELD signs. - To provide contrast with aesthetic surface treatments, a gap of at least one-half the width of the white transverse line, but not less than 6 inches, should be used between the white crosswalk lines and the aesthetic treatment (unmarked pavement or a black contrast line). Options (permitted): - High-visibility crosswalk markings may be used where additional conspicuity is desired for a crosswalk over transverse line crosswalk markings. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Section 3C.04 Guidance §1 (p.591) - Section 3C.05 Option §1 (p.591) - Section 3H.03 Guidance §11 (p.623) - Section 3C.01 Support §1 (p.589) - Figure 3C-2 (p.593) - Figure 3B-27 (p.586) - Page 631 - Page 632 - Page 664

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

**Rationale:** The response received 93.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. | A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4C.01, Section 2B.12, Section 4B.03, p.651, p.79, p.649. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. Adjudication note: Correct non-compulsion rule; omits the documented installation case where no warrant is met. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that meeting one or more traffic signal warrants does not by itself require installing a traffic control signal. Warrant satisfaction is only one factor in an engineering study, and agencies are encouraged to consider alternatives to signals even when warrants are met, since signals can sometimes increase delay or certain crash types. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - A parallel principle applies to stop control: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Guidance (recommended): - Because road user delay and the frequency of some types of crashes are sometimes higher under traffic signal control than under STOP sign control, consideration should be given to providing alternatives to traffic control signals even if one or more of the signal warrants has been satisfied. - Where a signal is installed based on projected (estimated) traffic volumes, an engineering study should be done within 1 year of putting the signal into steady (stop-and-go) operation to determine if the signal is justified (except where Warrant 8 was used); if not justified, the signal should be taken out of steady operation or removed. Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79) - Section 4B.03 Guidance §1 (p.649) - Section 4C.01 Guidance §12 (p.652) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653)

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

## T036

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, Section 4F.01, Section 4F.02, Section 4F.09, p.644, p.682. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN indication is permitted to proceed straight through, turn right, turn left, or make a U-turn, unless the movement is modified by other traffic control devices such as lane-use signs, turn prohibitions, lane markings, roadway design, or separate turn signal indications. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk and other vehicles lawfully within the intersection, and left-turning or U-turning drivers must additionally yield to closely approaching opposing traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." Options (permitted): - A steady straight-through GREEN ARROW signal indication may be used instead of a CIRCULAR GREEN in a signal face to discourage wrong-way turns under specified conditions (e.g., approaches intersecting a one-way street or an interchange exit ramp, unique geometric designs prohibiting turns, or approaches with pre-signals with separately controlled adjacent lanes), even if opposed by a simultaneous permissive left-turn movement. Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644) - Section 4F.01 Option §5 (p.682) - Section 4F.02 Support §2 (p.683) - Section 4F.09 Support §2 (p.693)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 4F.17, Section 4H.11, Section 4J.03, Section 4N.03, p.708, p.717. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Adjudication note: Correct function, engineering basis, and duration guidance; omits the prohibition on pre-yellow displays. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The yellow change interval's exclusive function is to warn approaching traffic that their permission to proceed is being terminated, after which they will be directed to stop (or must complete a turn permissively). Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with a recommended range of 3 to 6 seconds for hybrid beacons, the longer values reserved for higher-speed approaches. The evidence provided contains no provision addressing pre-yellow vehicular countdown displays, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." Guidance (recommended): - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons, a yellow change interval should have a minimum duration of 3 seconds and a maximum duration of 6 seconds, with the longer intervals reserved for use on approaches with higher speeds. Citations: - Section 4F.17 Support §1 (p.708) - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4H.11 Support §3 (p.717) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4N.03 Guidance §5 (p.743) Note: Support text explains that the yellow change interval's exclusive function is warning traffic of the termination of their permission to proceed; providing clearance time through the intersection is the purpose of the red clearance interval, not the yellow change interval. No evidence was provided regarding whether a pre-yellow vehicular countdown display may be used, so the MUTCD's position on that cannot be stated from this evidence.

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.

**Question:** What walking speed is generally used to calculate pedestrian clearance time, and what alternative speed may be used with an extended push-button feature?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Section 4K.05, p.724, p.725, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. | Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD recommends a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second for calculating the pedestrian clearance time, with an even slower speed considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Where an extended push button press feature is installed to give slower pedestrians extra time on request, a walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used instead. Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - "Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time." - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient for a pedestrian who left the pedestrian detector (or a location 6 feet behind the curb face or pavement edge if no detector is present) at the beginning of the WALK indication to cross at 3 feet per second to the far side of the traveled way, or to the median if a two-stage crossing sequence is used; any additional time needed should be added to the walk interval. Options (permitted): - "A walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the pedestrian clearance time at locations where an extended push button press function has been installed to provide slower pedestrians an opportunity to request and receive a longer pedestrian clearance time." Passive pedestrian detection may also be used to automatically adjust the clearance time based on actual walking speed or actual clearance of the crosswalk. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - An extended push button press may provide pedestrians with additional features such as increased crossing time, audible beaconing, or a speech push button information message. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §14 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Option §8 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4K.05 Option §1 (p.734)

**Gold answer:** The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory on every pedestrian signal head used at a crosswalk where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "Countdown pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.04) for the crosswalk or pedestrian crossing movement that crosses the approach shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation." - When provided, the countdown "shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication." Options (permitted): - Pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less may include a pedestrian change interval countdown display. Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 9E.12 Standard §8 (p.1105) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Option §2 (p.720)

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.

The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, the beacon must display, in order: a flashing CIRCULAR yellow indication, then a steady CIRCULAR yellow indication, then both steady CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian walk interval, then alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian change interval. When the pedestrian change interval ends, the beacon faces must go dark, and the beacon must remain dark between actuations. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Guidance (recommended): - If the pedestrian hybrid beacon is coordinated as a part of a signal system, it should remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation has been received until the point in the background cycle when the predetermined duration of the flashing yellow interval needs to be initiated in order to achieve the appropriate coordinated offset. Options (permitted): - If a minimum dark time between activations has been set on the controller, the beacon may remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation has been received until the minimum dark time has been provided. - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3 (steady yellow), and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Visual evidence: [Image 1] (Figure 4J-3) illustrates the six-step sequence — 1. Dark Until Activated, 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation, 3. Steady Yellow, 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval, 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval, and 6. Dark Again Until Activated — with a legend defining SY, FY, SR, and FR, and a note describing the optional steady red clearance and buffer intervals. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §7 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Option §8 (p.730) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself, with high visual contrast, aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk so pedestrians can identify the crossing it actuates and align themselves. A locator tone is also required in the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities find the arrow and push button, and the tone may be deactivated during DONT WALK display only if passive detection reactivates it when a pedestrian is within 12 feet. Standards (mandatory): - "each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." Guidance (recommended): - In temporary traffic control contexts, if a pushbutton is used to provide equivalent TTC information to pedestrians with vision disabilities, it should be equipped with a locator tone to notify pedestrians that a special accommodation is available and to help them locate the pushbutton. Options (permitted): - The push button locator tone may default to a deactivated mode during periods when the steady UPRAISED HAND (DONT WALK) indication is displayed, provided a passive pedestrian detection system activates the locator tone at all times (other than during the audible walk indication) that a pedestrian is present within a 12-foot radius of the push button; where pedestrian facilities such as sidewalks are present, the passive detection requirement may be limited to pedestrians on those facilities within the 12-foot radius. Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 6C.03 Guidance §8 (p.781) - Section 4K.04 Option §5 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Support §3 (p.734) - Section 4K.01 Support §11 (p.731)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4S.01, Section 4I.06, Section 4L.03, Section 4L.01, Section 4L.02, p.738. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. | When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs must remain dark until actuated by a pedestrian, then flash for a predetermined period (or, with passive detection, until the pedestrian clears the crosswalk). All RRFB units serving a given crosswalk must start and stop flashing simultaneously, and when activated they must flash at a rapid rate of 75 flashing sequences per minute. The flashing duration should be timed based on pedestrian clearance time procedures. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." Guidance (recommended): - The minimum duration of the predetermined period of RRFB operation following each actuation should be based on the procedures for timing pedestrian clearance times for pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.06). Options (permitted): - A pedestrian-activated and/or bicyclist-activated RRFB may be used to provide supplemental emphasis to pedestrian, school, and trail warning signs at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches. - At intersections with two crosswalks on an uncontrolled approach, post-mounted RRFBs may be installed to face only one direction of travel at the first crosswalk that traffic encounters; when activated, the RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease their rapid flashing operation. Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Guidance §3 (p.738) - Section 4L.01 Option §1 (p.736) - Section 4L.02 Option §11 (p.738)

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6A.02, Section 8A.13, Section 6A.01, Section 1A.01, p.766, p.768. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicycli… Adjudication note: Correct high-priority lifecycle and user coverage; rail-transit coordination is not stated in the direct answer. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes that the safety and accessibility of both road users and workers should be an integral, high-priority element of every project involving temporary traffic control (TTC), from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers, and is reflected in the seven fundamental principles of TTC. Note that these provisions are Guidance (recommended), not mandatory Standards. Guidance (recommended): - "Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction." Maintenance and utility work should likewise be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers considered at all times, and early coordination with the railroad or light rail transit agency should occur if the TTC zone includes a grade crossing. - General plans or guidelines should be developed to provide safety for motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians, workers, and enforcement/emergency officials; the basic safety principles governing permanent roadways should also govern TTC zone design, with the goal of routing road users through TTC zones using geometrics, roadside features, and devices as comparable as possible to normal highway conditions; a TTC plan appropriate to the project's complexity should be prepared and understood before the site is occupied, with changes approved by a knowledgeable official; and road user movement should be inhibited as little as practical, avoiding frequent and abrupt geometric changes. - Temporary traffic control operations should minimize inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic, and prior notice should be given to affected agencies, emergency services, businesses, railroad companies or transit agencies, and road users before free movement is infringed upon or blocked. Support (informational context): - TTC planning provides for continuity of motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian traffic movement (including accessible passage), transit operations, and access (and accessibility) to property and utilities when the normal function of a roadway is suspended. - At grade crossings, TTC planning similarly provides for continuity of operations when the normal roadway function is suspended, and is also needed when traffic is detoured over an existing grade crossing. - The MUTCD's overall purpose is to establish uniform national criteria for traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all facilities open to public travel. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766) - Section 6A.02 Guidance §2 (p.768) - Section 8A.13 Guidance §7 (p.992) - Section 6A.01 Support §3 (p.765) - Section 8A.13 Support §1 (p.992) - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to… | The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.951 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: All workers within the right-of-way who are within a TTC zone, including emergency responders, must wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 for both daytime and nighttime activity, with the appropriate class selected by an employer-designated safety person. This requirement extends to uniformed law enforcement directing traffic and to firefighters and other emergency responders, though responders may alternatively wear ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 public safety vests. Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Guidance (recommended): - For flagger wear during nighttime activity, apparel meeting the Performance Class 3 requirements of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 (or equivalent revision), and labeled as meeting the ANSI 107-2015 standard performance for Class 3 risk exposure, should be worn. - All on-scene responders and news media personnel should constantly be aware of their visibility to oncoming traffic and wear high-visibility apparel; on-scene responder organizations should train their personnel in TTC practices. Options (permitted): - Emergency and incident responders and law enforcement personnel within the TTC zone may wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting the performance requirements of ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 ("American National Standard for High-Visibility Public Safety Vests"), or equivalent revisions, and labeled as ANSI 207-2006, in lieu of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 apparel. Citations: - Section 6C.05 Standard §1 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §3 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §5 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Guidance §7 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §7 (p.854) - Section 6C.05 Option §4 (p.782)

**Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD recommends (as Guidance, not a mandatory Standard) that flaggers be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques, and that they be able to satisfactorily demonstrate five specific abilities. These cover communication, quick movement to avoid danger, control of signaling devices, application of proper traffic control practices, and recognizing dangerous traffic situations in time to warn workers. Guidance (recommended): - Because flaggers are responsible for public safety and make the greatest number of contacts with the public of all highway workers, they should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques. - Flaggers should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate: (A) ability to receive and communicate specific instructions clearly, firmly, and courteously; (B) ability to move and maneuver quickly in order to avoid danger from errant vehicles; (C) ability to control signaling devices (such as paddles and flags) in order to provide clear and positive guidance to drivers approaching a TTC zone in frequently changing situations; (D) ability to understand and apply proper traffic control practices, sometimes in stressful or emergency situations; and (E) ability to recognize dangerous traffic situations and warn workers in sufficient time to avoid injury. - Where manual traffic control is needed, it should be provided by qualified flaggers or uniformed law enforcement officers. Citations: - Section 6D.01 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §16 (p.855)

**Gold answer:** A flagger should communicate instructions clearly and courteously; move quickly to avoid danger; control paddles or flags to give clear guidance; understand and apply traffic-control practices under stress; and recognize dangerous situations and warn workers in time.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end of the section must be coordinated, except in the limited self-regulating case allowed by the MUTCD. Control may be provided by flaggers (one or two, depending on visibility), by temporary traffic control signals, or — on short, low-volume sections with through visibility — the movement may be self-regulating. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - For two-lane, two-way traffic on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, "opposing vehicular traffic shall be separated with either temporary traffic barriers (concrete safety-shape or approved alternate), channelizing devices, Narrow Two-Way Traffic (W6-4) signs on flexible supports (see Section 6H.17), or a temporary raised island throughout the length of the two-way operation. The use of markings and complementary signing, by themselves, shall not be used." Options (permitted): - When a one-lane, two-way TTC zone is short enough for a flagger to see from one end of the zone to the other, traffic may be controlled by either a single flagger or by a flagger at each end of the section. - If the work space on a low-volume street or road is short and road users from both directions can see traffic approaching from the opposite direction through and beyond the worksite, the movement through the one-lane, two-way constriction may be self-regulating. - Traffic control signals may be used to control vehicular traffic movements in one-lane, two-way TTC zones. Support (informational): - Techniques for controlling vehicular traffic under one-lane, two-way conditions on a two-lane highway are described in Section 6E.01. Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6N.14 Standard §2 (p.850) - Section 6E.02 Option §2 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Option §4 (p.787) - Section 6E.05 Option §1 (p.788) - Section 6N.09 Support §6 (p.846)

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

A School (S1-1) sign is required in advance of the first School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 sign in each approach direction, using the advance placement guidelines in Table 2C-3. The downstream end of the zone is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, though an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign may be used where the zones end at the same point. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." - "The School Speed Limit assembly shall be either a static sign assembly, a blank-out sign, or a changeable message sign (see Chapter 2L)." Options (permitted): - "The downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign (see Figures 7B-1 and 7B-2)" — i.e., where the reduced school speed limit zone ends at the same point as the designated school zone, the S5-2 sign may serve as the terminal sign. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 7B-1 illustrates the School Speed Limit assembly components (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 Speed Limit sign, and S4-1P/S4-2P/S4-4P/S4-6P bottom plaques), the S5-1 When Flashing sign, and the END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) and END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) signs. [Image 2] Table 2C-3 provides the advance placement distances used to locate the School (S1-1) sign upstream of the assembly. [Image 3] Figure 7B-2 shows an example layout with the S1-1 sign in advance, the School Speed Limit assembly at the zone's beginning, and the S5-3 or S5-2 sign marking the downstream end. [Image 4] Figure 7B-4 shows the same placement pattern for a school zone with a school speed limit and higher fines only for speeding, including S5-3/S5-2 terminal signing. [Images 5–8] These full-page views reproduce the relevant Chapter 7B text and figure content confirming the placement, advance signing, and end-of-zone requirements. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §6 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Option §7 (p.976) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979)

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must have a depth of at least 10 feet (measured from its upstream edge nearest the motor vehicle stop line to its downstream edge nearest the crosswalk or intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Green-colored pavement is optional, but if used it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box, and bicycle boxes are one of the limited applications for which green-colored pavement is allowed. The evidence provided does not include specific operational restrictions (e.g., signal-phasing or turn restrictions) for bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes..., B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections..., C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane in which motor vehicles must weave across bicyclists in bicycle lanes..., D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes..., E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols..." Guidance (recommended): - If green-colored pavement is used within separated bicycle lanes on an independent alignment, it should be used only at the entrances to those facilities from roadways open to public travel or at conflict, weaving, or crossing locations. (This guidance concerns separated bicycle lanes, not bicycle boxes themselves.) Options (permitted): - Green-colored pavement may be installed for the entire length of a bicycle lane or bicycle lane extension or for only a portion (or portions) of it — but note that within a bicycle box, partial coverage is not permitted; full coverage is required per the Standard above. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Guidance §5 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Option §7 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Support §1 (p.625)

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

Options (permitted): - All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates specified crash experience (e.g., five or more reported crashes in 12 months at a four-leg intersection, of a type susceptible to correction by all-way stop control)—again a threshold for consideration, not a promised reduction. - All-way stop control may also be installed based on inadequate sight distance, as an interim measure pending signal or roundabout yield control, or based on 8-hour combined vehicle/pedestrian/bicycle volumes; none of these provisions cite any crash-reduction percentage. The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - "Speed zones (other than statutory speed limits) shall only be established on the basis of an engineering study that has been performed in accordance with traffic engineering practices. The engineering study shall consider the roadway context." Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-1 need to be abbreviated in connection with traffic control devices, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-1 shall be used." Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." - Recommended alternatives to back-mounting that would obscure an exclusive shape include: installing signs on separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height to fit a back-mounted sign below its bottom edge. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-1, which pairs the octagon with Stop (R1-1), the circle with Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and the pennant with No Passing Zone (W14-3). ## TB020

**Question:** What is the key difference between automation Levels 3 and 4 in Table 5A-1 regarding a human response to a request to intervene?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, p.760. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… | At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=0.25.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Table 5A-1, the distinction hinges on whether the system depends on the human driver as a fallback. At Level 3, the Automated Driving System (ADS) performs the Dynamic Driving Task within a given Operational Design Domain "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene." At Level 4, the ADS continues to perform the Dynamic Driving Task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — meaning the vehicle does not rely on the human to take over. This material is descriptive (Support), not a mandatory Standard. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, where Level 3 is described as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," and Level 4 as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task, even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene"; both levels fall under the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760)

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.
